"c0e2a25feb2068a3f3bd2b0dae31f1f1e090e470631df574f92f4ceb935b37e5", "reading_scope": "excerpt", "category": "source_original", "content": "[EXCERPT extracted-character range 0:26288]\n\n===== Doc/library/subprocess.rst =====\n:mod:`!subprocess` --- Subprocess management\n============================================\n\n.. module:: subprocess\n   :synopsis: Subprocess management.\n\n**Source code:** :source:`Lib/subprocess.py`\n\n--------------\n\nThe :mod:`!subprocess` module allows you to spawn new processes, connect to their\ninput/output/error pipes, and obtain their return codes.  This module intends to\nreplace several older modules and functions::\n\n   os.system\n   os.spawn*\n\nInformation about how the :mod:`!subprocess` module can be used to replace these\nmodules and functions can be found in the following sections.\n\n.. seealso::\n\n   :pep:`324` -- PEP proposing the subprocess module\n\n.. include:: ../includes/wasm-mobile-notavail.rst\n\nUsing the :mod:`!subprocess` Module\n-----------------------------------\n\nThe recommended approach to invoking subprocesses is to use the :func:`run`\nfunction for all use cases it can handle. For more advanced use cases, the\nunderlying :class:`Popen` interface can be used directly.\n\n\n.. function:: run(args, *, stdin=None, input=None, stdout=None, stderr=None,\\\n                  capture_output=False, shell=False, cwd=None, timeout=None, \\\n                  check=False, encoding=None, errors=None, text=None, env=None, \\\n                  universal_newlines=None, **other_popen_kwargs)\n\n   Run the command described by *args*.  Wait for command to complete, then\n   return a :class:`CompletedProcess` instance.\n\n   The arguments shown above are merely the most common ones, described below\n   in :ref:`frequently-used-arguments` (hence the use of keyword-only notation\n   in the abbreviated signature). The full function signature is largely the\n   same as that of the :class:`Popen` constructor - most of the arguments to\n   this function are passed through to that interface. (*timeout*,  *input*,\n   *check*, and *capture_output* are not.)\n\n   If *capture_output* is true, stdout and stderr will be captured.\n   When used, the internal :class:`Popen` object is automatically created with\n   *stdout* and *stderr* both set to :data:`~subprocess.PIPE`.\n   The *stdout* and *stderr* arguments may not be supplied at the same time as *capture_output*.\n   If you wish to capture and combine both streams into one,\n   set *stdout* to :data:`~subprocess.PIPE`\n   and *stderr* to :data:`~subprocess.STDOUT`,\n   instead of using *capture_output*.\n\n   A *timeout* may be specified in seconds, it is internally passed on to\n   :meth:`Popen.communicate`. If the timeout expires, the child process will be\n   killed and waited for. The :exc:`TimeoutExpired` exception will be\n   re-raised after the child process has terminated. The initial process\n   creation itself cannot be interrupted on many platform APIs so you are not\n   guaranteed to see a timeout exception until at least after however long\n   process creation takes.\n\n   The *input* argument is passed to :meth:`Popen.communicate` and thus to the\n   subprocess's stdin.  If used it must be a byte sequence, or a string if\n   *encoding* or *errors* is specified or *text* is true.  When\n   used, the internal :class:`Popen` object is automatically created with\n   *stdin* set to :data:`~subprocess.PIPE`,\n   and the *stdin* argument may not be used as well.\n\n   If *check* is true, and the process exits with a non-zero exit code, a\n   :exc:`CalledProcessError` exception will be raised. Attributes of that\n   exception hold the arguments, the exit code, and stdout and stderr if they\n   were captured.\n\n   If *encoding* or *errors* are specified, or *text* is true,\n   file objects for stdin, stdout and stderr are opened in text mode using the\n   specified *encoding* and *errors* or the :class:`io.TextIOWrapper` default.\n   The *universal_newlines* argument is equivalent  to *text* and is provided\n   for backwards compatibility. By default, file objects are opened in binary mode.\n\n   If *env* is not ``None``, it must be a mapping that defines the environment\n   variables for the new process; these are used instead of the default\n   behavior of inheriting the current process' environment. It is passed\n   directly to :class:`Popen`. This mapping can be str to str on any platform\n   or bytes to bytes on POSIX platforms much like :data:`os.environ` or\n   :data:`os.environb`.\n\n   Examples::\n\n      >>> subprocess.run([\"ls\", \"-l\"])  # doesn't capture output\n      CompletedProcess(args=['ls', '-l'], returncode=0)\n\n      >>> subprocess.run(\"exit 1\", shell=True, check=True)\n      Traceback (most recent call last):\n        ...\n      subprocess.CalledProcessError: Command 'exit 1' returned non-zero exit status 1\n\n      >>> subprocess.run([\"ls\", \"-l\", \"/dev/null\"], capture_output=True)\n      CompletedProcess(args=['ls', '-l', '/dev/null'], returncode=0,\n      stdout=b'crw-rw-rw- 1 root root 1, 3 Jan 23 16:23 /dev/null\\n', stderr=b'')\n\n   .. versionadded:: 3.5\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.6\n\n      Added *encoding* and *errors* parameters\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.7\n\n      Added the *text* parameter, as a more understandable alias of *universal_newlines*.\n      Added the *capture_output* parameter.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.12\n\n      Changed Windows shell search order for ``shell=True``. The current\n      directory and ``%PATH%`` are replaced with ``%COMSPEC%`` and\n      ``%SystemRoot%\\System32\\cmd.exe``. As a result, dropping a\n      malicious program named ``cmd.exe`` into a current directory no\n      longer works.\n\n.. class:: CompletedProcess\n\n   The return value from :func:`run`, representing a process that has finished.\n\n   .. attribute:: args\n\n      The arguments used to launch the process. This may be a list or a string.\n\n   .. attribute:: returncode\n\n      Exit status of the child process. Typically, an exit status of 0 indicates\n      that it ran successfully.\n\n      A negative value ``-N`` indicates that the child was terminated by signal\n      ``N`` (POSIX only).\n\n   .. attribute:: stdout\n\n      Captured stdout from the child process. A bytes sequence, or a string if\n      :func:`run` was called with an encoding, errors, or text=True.\n      ``None`` if stdout was not captured.\n\n      If you ran the process with ``stderr=subprocess.STDOUT``, stdout and\n      stderr will be combined in this attribute, and :attr:`stderr` will be\n      ``None``.\n\n   .. attribute:: stderr\n\n      Captured stderr from the child process. A bytes sequence, or a string if\n      :func:`run` was called with an encoding, errors, or text=True.\n      ``None`` if stderr was not captured.\n\n   .. method:: check_returncode()\n\n      If :attr:`returncode` is non-zero, raise a :exc:`CalledProcessError`.\n\n   .. versionadded:: 3.5\n\n.. data:: DEVNULL\n\n   Special value that can be used as the *stdin*, *stdout* or *stderr* argument\n   to :class:`Popen` and indicates that the special file :data:`os.devnull`\n   will be used.\n\n   .. versionadded:: 3.3\n\n\n.. data:: PIPE\n\n   Special value that can be used as the *stdin*, *stdout* or *stderr* argument\n   to :class:`Popen` and indicates that a pipe to the standard stream should be\n   opened.  Most useful with :meth:`Popen.communicate`.\n\n\n.. data:: STDOUT\n\n   Special value that can be used as the *stderr* argument to :class:`Popen` and\n   indicates that standard error should go into the same handle as standard\n   output.\n\n\n.. exception:: SubprocessError\n\n    Base class for all other exceptions from this module.\n\n    .. versionadded:: 3.3\n\n\n.. exception:: TimeoutExpired\n\n    Subclass of :exc:`SubprocessError`, raised when a timeout expires\n    while waiting for a child process.\n\n    .. attribute:: cmd\n\n        Command that was used to spawn the child process.\n\n    .. attribute:: timeout\n\n        Timeout in seconds.\n\n    .. attribute:: output\n\n        Output of the child process if it was captured by :func:`run` or\n        :func:`check_output`.  Otherwise, ``None``.  This is always\n        :class:`bytes` when any output was captured regardless of the\n        ``text=True`` setting.  It may remain ``None`` instead of ``b''``\n        when no output was observed.\n\n    .. attribute:: stdout\n\n        Alias for output, for symmetry with :attr:`stderr`.\n\n    .. attribute:: stderr\n\n        Stderr output of the child process if it was captured by :func:`run`.\n        Otherwise, ``None``.  This is always :class:`bytes` when stderr output\n        was captured regardless of the ``text=True`` setting.  It may remain\n        ``None`` instead of ``b''`` when no stderr output was observed.\n\n    .. versionadded:: 3.3\n\n    .. versionchanged:: 3.5\n        *stdout* and *stderr* attributes added\n\n.. exception:: CalledProcessError\n\n    Subclass of :exc:`SubprocessError`, raised when a process run by\n    :func:`check_call`, :func:`check_output`, or :func:`run` (with ``check=True``)\n    returns a non-zero exit status.\n\n\n    .. attribute:: returncode\n\n        Exit status of the child process.  If the process exited due to a\n        signal, this will be the negative signal number.\n\n    .. attribute:: cmd\n\n        Command that was used to spawn the child process.\n\n    .. attribute:: output\n\n        Output of the child process if it was captured by :func:`run` or\n        :func:`check_output`.  Otherwise, ``None``.\n\n    .. attribute:: stdout\n\n        Alias for output, for symmetry with :attr:`stderr`.\n\n    .. attribute:: stderr\n\n        Stderr output of the child process if it was captured by :func:`run`.\n        Otherwise, ``None``.\n\n    .. versionchanged:: 3.5\n        *stdout* and *stderr* attributes added\n\n\n.. _frequently-used-arguments:\n\nFrequently Used Arguments\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\nTo support a wide variety of use cases, the :class:`Popen` constructor (and\nthe convenience functions) accept a large number of optional arguments. For\nmost typical use cases, many of these arguments can be safely left at their\ndefault values. The arguments that are most commonly needed are:\n\n   *args* is required for all calls and should be a string, or a sequence of\n   program arguments. Providing a sequence of arguments is generally\n   preferred, as it allows the module to take care of any required escaping\n   and quoting of arguments (e.g. to permit spaces in file names). If passing\n   a single string, either *shell* must be :const:`True` (see below) or else\n   the string must simply name the program to be executed without specifying\n   any arguments.\n\n   *stdin*, *stdout* and *stderr* specify the executed program's standard input,\n   standard output and standard error file handles, respectively.  Valid values\n   are ``None``, :data:`PIPE`, :data:`DEVNULL`, an existing file descriptor (a\n   positive integer), and an existing :term:`file object` with a valid file\n   descriptor.  With the default settings of ``None``, no redirection will\n   occur.  :data:`PIPE` indicates that a new pipe to the child should be\n   created.  :data:`DEVNULL` indicates that the special file :data:`os.devnull`\n   will be used.  Additionally, *stderr* can be :data:`STDOUT`, which indicates\n   that the stderr data from the child process should be captured into the same\n   file handle as for *stdout*.\n\n   .. index::\n      single: universal newlines; subprocess module\n\n   If *encoding* or *errors* are specified, or *text* (also known as\n   *universal_newlines*) is true,\n   the file objects *stdin*, *stdout* and *stderr* will be opened in text\n   mode using the *encoding* and *errors* specified in the call or the\n   defaults for :class:`io.TextIOWrapper`.\n\n   For *stdin*, line ending characters ``'\\n'`` in the input will be converted\n   to the default line separator :data:`os.linesep`. For *stdout* and *stderr*,\n   all line endings in the output will be converted to ``'\\n'``.  For more\n   information see the documentation of the :class:`io.TextIOWrapper` class\n   when the *newline* argument to its constructor is ``None``.\n\n   If text mode is not used, *stdin*, *stdout* and *stderr* will be opened as\n   binary streams. No encoding or line ending conversion is performed.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.6\n      Added the *encoding* and *errors* parameters.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.7\n      Added the *text* parameter as an alias for *universal_newlines*.\n\n   .. note::\n\n      The newlines attribute of the file objects :attr:`Popen.stdin`,\n      :attr:`Popen.stdout` and :attr:`Popen.stderr` are not updated by\n      the :meth:`Popen.communicate` method.\n\n   If *shell* is ``True``, the specified command will be executed through\n   the shell.  This can be useful if you are using Python primarily for the\n   enhanced control flow it offers over most system shells and still want\n   convenient access to other shell features such as shell pipes, filename\n   wildcards, environment variable expansion, and expansion of ``~`` to a\n   user's home directory.  However, note that Python itself offers\n   implementations of many shell-like features (in particular, :mod:`glob`,\n   :mod:`fnmatch`, :func:`os.walk`, :func:`os.path.expandvars`,\n   :func:`os.path.expanduser`, and :mod:`shutil`).\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.3\n      When *universal_newlines* is ``True``, the class uses the encoding\n      :func:`locale.getpreferredencoding(False) <locale.getpreferredencoding>`\n      instead of ``locale.getpreferredencoding()``.  See the\n      :class:`io.TextIOWrapper` class for more information on this change.\n\n   .. note::\n\n      Read the `Security Considerations`_ section before using ``shell=True``.\n\nThese options, along with all of the other options, are described in more\ndetail in the :class:`Popen` constructor documentation.\n\n\nPopen Constructor\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\nThe underlying process creation and management in this module is handled by\nthe :class:`Popen` class. It offers a lot of flexibility so that developers\nare able to handle the less common cases not covered by the convenience\nfunctions.\n\n\n.. class:: Popen(args, bufsize=-1, executable=None, stdin=None, stdout=None, \\\n                 stderr=None, preexec_fn=None, close_fds=True, shell=False, \\\n                 cwd=None, env=None, universal_newlines=None, \\\n                 startupinfo=None, creationflags=0, restore_signals=True, \\\n                 start_new_session=False, pass_fds=(), *, group=None, \\\n                 extra_groups=None, user=None, umask=-1, \\\n                 encoding=None, errors=None, text=None, pipesize=-1, \\\n                 process_group=None)\n\n   Execute a child program in a new process.  On POSIX, the class uses\n   :meth:`os.execvpe`-like behavior to execute the child program.  On Windows,\n   the class uses the Windows ``CreateProcess()`` function.  The arguments to\n   :class:`Popen` are as follows.\n\n   *args* should be a sequence of program arguments or else a single string\n   or :term:`path-like object`.\n   By default, the program to execute is the first item in *args* if *args* is\n   a sequence.  If *args* is a string, the interpretation is\n   platform-dependent and described below.  See the *shell* and *executable*\n   arguments for additional differences from the default behavior.  Unless\n   otherwise stated, it is recommended to pass *args* as a sequence.\n\n   .. warning::\n\n      For maximum reliability, use a fully qualified path for the executable.\n      To search for an unqualified name on :envvar:`PATH`, use\n      :meth:`shutil.which`. On all platforms, passing :data:`sys.executable`\n      is the recommended way to launch the current Python interpreter again,\n      and use the ``-m`` command-line format to launch an installed module.\n\n      Resolving the path of *executable* (or the first item of *args*) is\n      platform dependent. For POSIX, see :meth:`os.execvpe`, and note that\n      when resolving or searching for the executable path, *cwd* overrides the\n      current working directory and *env* can override the ``PATH``\n      environment variable. For Windows, see the documentation of the\n      ``lpApplicationName`` and ``lpCommandLine`` parameters of WinAPI\n      ``CreateProcess``, and note that when resolving or searching for the\n      executable path with ``shell=False``, *cwd* does not override the\n      current working directory and *env* cannot override the ``PATH``\n      environment variable. Using a full path avoids all of these variations.\n\n   An example of passing some arguments to an external program\n   as a sequence is::\n\n     Popen([\"/usr/bin/git\", \"commit\", \"-m\", \"Fixes a bug.\"])\n\n   On POSIX, if *args* is a string, the string is interpreted as the name or\n   path of the program to execute.  However, this can only be done if not\n   passing arguments to the program.\n\n   .. note::\n\n      It may not be obvious how to break a shell command into a sequence of arguments,\n      especially in complex cases. :meth:`shlex.split` can illustrate how to\n      determine the correct tokenization for *args*::\n\n         >>> import shlex, subprocess\n         >>> command_line = input()\n         /bin/vikings -input eggs.txt -output \"spam spam.txt\" -cmd \"echo '$MONEY'\"\n         >>> args = shlex.split(command_line)\n         >>> print(args)\n         ['/bin/vikings', '-input', 'eggs.txt', '-output', 'spam spam.txt', '-cmd', \"echo '$MONEY'\"]\n         >>> p = subprocess.Popen(args) # Success!\n\n      Note in particular that options (such as *-input*) and arguments (such\n      as *eggs.txt*) that are separated by whitespace in the shell go in separate\n      list elements, while arguments that need quoting or backslash escaping when\n      used in the shell (such as filenames containing spaces or the *echo* command\n      shown above) are single list elements.\n\n   On Windows, if *args* is a sequence, it will be converted to a string in a\n   manner described in :ref:`converting-argument-sequence`.  This is because\n   the underlying ``CreateProcess()`` operates on strings.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.6\n      *args* parameter accepts a :term:`path-like object` if *shell* is\n      ``False`` and a sequence containing path-like objects on POSIX.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.8\n      *args* parameter accepts a :term:`path-like object` if *shell* is\n      ``False`` and a sequence containing bytes and path-like objects\n      on Windows.\n\n   The *shell* argument (which defaults to ``False``) specifies whether to use\n   the shell as the program to execute.  If *shell* is ``True``, it is\n   recommended to pass *args* as a string rather than as a sequence.\n\n   On POSIX with ``shell=True``, the shell defaults to :file:`/bin/sh`.  If\n   *args* is a string, the string specifies the command\n   to execute through the shell.  This means that the string must be\n   formatted exactly as it would be when typed at the shell prompt.  This\n   includes, for example, quoting or backslash escaping filenames with spaces in\n   them.  If *args* is a sequence, the first item specifies the command string, and\n   any additional items will be treated as additional arguments to the shell\n   itself.  That is to say, :class:`Popen` does the equivalent of::\n\n      Popen(['/bin/sh', '-c', args[0], args[1], ...])\n\n   On Windows with ``shell=True``, the :envvar:`COMSPEC` environment variable\n   specifies the default shell.  The only time you need to specify\n   ``shell=True`` on Windows is when the command you wish to execute is built\n   into the shell (e.g. :command:`dir` or :command:`copy`).  You do not need\n   ``shell=True`` to run a batch file or console-based executable.\n\n   .. note::\n\n      Read the `Security Considerations`_ section before using ``shell=True``.\n\n   *bufsize* will be supplied as the corresponding argument to the\n   :func:`open` function when creating the stdin/stdout/stderr pipe\n   file objects:\n\n   - ``0`` means unbuffered (read and write are one\n     system call and can return short)\n   - ``1`` means line buffered\n     (only usable if ``text=True`` or ``universal_newlines=True``)\n   - any other positive value means use a buffer of approximately that\n     size\n   - negative bufsize (the default) means the system default of\n     io.DEFAULT_BUFFER_SIZE will be used.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.3.1\n      *bufsize* now defaults to -1 to enable buffering by default to match the\n      behavior that most code expects.  In versions prior to Python 3.2.4 and\n      3.3.1 it incorrectly defaulted to ``0`` which was unbuffered\n      and allowed short reads.  This was unintentional and did not match the\n      behavior of Python 2 as most code expected.\n\n   The *executable* argument specifies a replacement program to execute.   It\n   is very seldom needed.  When ``shell=False``, *executable* replaces the\n   program to execute specified by *args*.  However, the original *args* is\n   still passed to the program.  Most programs treat the program specified\n   by *args* as the command name, which can then be different from the program\n   actually executed.  On POSIX, the *args* name\n   becomes the display name for the executable in utilities such as\n   :program:`ps`.  If ``shell=True``, on POSIX the *executable* argument\n   specifies a replacement shell for the default :file:`/bin/sh`.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.6\n      *executable* parameter accepts a :term:`path-like object` on POSIX.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.8\n      *executable* parameter accepts a bytes and :term:`path-like object`\n      on Windows.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.12\n\n      Changed Windows shell search order for ``shell=True``. The current\n      directory and ``%PATH%`` are replaced with ``%COMSPEC%`` and\n      ``%SystemRoot%\\System32\\cmd.exe``. As a result, dropping a\n      malicious program named ``cmd.exe`` into a current directory no\n      longer works.\n\n   *stdin*, *stdout* and *stderr* specify the executed program's standard input,\n   standard output and standard error file handles, respectively.  Valid values\n   are ``None``, :data:`PIPE`, :data:`DEVNULL`, an existing file descriptor (a\n   positive integer), and an existing :term:`file object` with a valid file\n   descriptor.  With the default settings of ``None``, no redirection will\n   occur.  :data:`PIPE` indicates that a new pipe to the child should be\n   created.  :data:`DEVNULL` indicates that the special file :data:`os.devnull`\n   will be used.  Additionally, *stderr* can be :data:`STDOUT`, which indicates\n   that the stderr data from the applications should be captured into the same\n   file handle as for *stdout*.\n\n   If *preexec_fn* is set to a callable object, this object will be called in the\n   child process just before the child is executed.\n   (POSIX only)\n\n   .. warning::\n\n      The *preexec_fn* parameter is NOT SAFE to use in the presence of threads\n      in your application.  The child process could deadlock before exec is\n      called.\n\n   .. note::\n\n      If you need to modify the environment for the child use the *env*\n      parameter rather than doing it in a *preexec_fn*.\n      The *start_new_session* and *process_group* parameters should take the place of\n      code using *preexec_fn* to call :func:`os.setsid` or :func:`os.setpgid` in the child.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.8\n\n      The *preexec_fn* parameter is no longer supported in subinterpreters.\n      The use of the parameter in a subinterpreter raises\n      :exc:`RuntimeError`. The new restriction may affect applications that\n      are deployed in mod_wsgi, uWSGI, and other embedded environments.\n\n   If *close_fds* is true, all file descriptors except ``0``, ``1`` and\n   ``2`` will be closed before the child process is executed.  Otherwise\n   when *close_fds* is false, file descriptors obey their inheritable flag\n   as described in :ref:`fd_inheritance`.\n\n   On Windows, if *close_fds* is true then no handles will be inherited by the\n   child process unless explicitly passed in the ``handle_list`` element of\n   :attr:`STARTUPINFO.lpAttributeList`, or by standard handle redirection.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.2\n      The default for *close_fds* was changed from :const:`False` to\n      what is described above.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.7\n      On Windows the default for *close_fds* was changed from :const:`False` to\n      :const:`True` when redirecting the standard handles. It's now possible to\n      set *close_fds* to :const:`True` when redirecting the standard handles.\n\n   *pass_fds* is an optional sequence of file descriptors to keep open\n   between the parent and child.  Providing any *pass_fds* forces\n   *close_fds* to be :const:`True`.  (POSIX only)\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.2\n      The *pass_fds* parameter was added.\n\n   If *cwd* is not ``None``, the function changes the working directory to\n   *cwd* before executing the child.  *cwd* can be a string, bytes or\n   :term:`path-like <path-like object>` object.  On POSIX, the function\n   looks for *executable* (or for the first item in *args*) relative to *cwd*\n   if the executable path is a relative path.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.6\n      *cwd* parameter accepts a :term:`path-like object` on POSIX.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.7\n      *cwd* parameter accepts a :term:`path-like object` on Windows.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.8\n      *cwd* parameter accepts a bytes object on Windows.\n\n   If *restore_signals* is true (the default) all signals that Python has set to\n   SIG_IGN are restored to SIG_DFL in the child process before the exec.\n   Currently this includes the SIGPIPE, SIGXFZ and SIGXFSZ signals.\n   (POSIX only)\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.2\n      *restore_signals* was added.\n\n   If *start_new_session* is true the ``setsid()`` system call will be made in the\n   child process prior to the execution of the subprocess.\n\n   .. availability:: POSIX\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.2\n      *start_new_session* was added.\n\n   If *process_group* is a non-negative integer, the ``setpgid(0, value)`` system call will\n   be made in the child process prior to the execution of the subprocess.\n\n   .. availability:: POSIX\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.11\n      *process_group* was added.\n\n   If *group* is not ``None``, the setregid() system call will be made in the\n   child process prior to the execution of the subprocess. If the provided\n   value is a string, it will be looked up via :func:`grp.getgrnam` and\n   the value in ``gr_gid`` will be used. If the value is an integer, it\n   will be passed verbatim. (POSIX only)\n\n   .. availability:: POSIX\n   .. versionadded:: 3.9\n\n   If *extra_groups* is not ``None``, the setgroups() system call will be\n   made in the child process prior to the execution of th\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 28000:40000]\ne-by-Side_Assembly\n\n   If *encoding* or *errors* are specified, or *text* is true, the file objects\n   *stdin*, *stdout* and *stderr* are opened in text mode with the specified\n   *encoding* and *errors*, as described above in :ref:`frequently-used-arguments`.\n   The *universal_newlines* argument is equivalent  to *text* and is provided\n   for backwards compatibility. By default, file objects are opened in binary mode.\n\n   .. versionadded:: 3.6\n      *encoding* and *errors* were added.\n\n   .. versionadded:: 3.7\n      *text* was added as a more readable alias for *universal_newlines*.\n\n   If given, *startupinfo* will be a :class:`STARTUPINFO` object, which is\n   passed to the underlying ``CreateProcess`` function.\n\n   If given, *creationflags*, can be one or more of the following flags:\n\n   * :data:`CREATE_NEW_CONSOLE`\n   * :data:`CREATE_NEW_PROCESS_GROUP`\n   * :data:`ABOVE_NORMAL_PRIORITY_CLASS`\n   * :data:`BELOW_NORMAL_PRIORITY_CLASS`\n   * :data:`HIGH_PRIORITY_CLASS`\n   * :data:`IDLE_PRIORITY_CLASS`\n   * :data:`NORMAL_PRIORITY_CLASS`\n   * :data:`REALTIME_PRIORITY_CLASS`\n   * :data:`CREATE_NO_WINDOW`\n   * :data:`DETACHED_PROCESS`\n   * :data:`CREATE_DEFAULT_ERROR_MODE`\n   * :data:`CREATE_BREAKAWAY_FROM_JOB`\n\n   *pipesize* can be used to change the size of the pipe when\n   :data:`PIPE` is used for *stdin*, *stdout* or *stderr*. The size of the pipe\n   is only changed on platforms that support this (only Linux at this time of\n   writing). Other platforms will ignore this parameter.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.10\n      Added the *pipesize* parameter.\n\n   Popen objects are supported as context managers via the :keyword:`with` statement:\n   on exit, standard file descriptors are closed, and the process is waited for.\n   ::\n\n      with Popen([\"ifconfig\"], stdout=PIPE) as proc:\n          log.write(proc.stdout.read())\n\n   .. audit-event:: subprocess.Popen executable,args,cwd,env subprocess.Popen\n\n      Popen and the other functions in this module that use it raise an\n      :ref:`auditing event <auditing>` ``subprocess.Popen`` with arguments\n      ``executable``, ``args``, ``cwd``, and ``env``. The value for ``args``\n      may be a single string or a list of strings, depending on platform.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.2\n      Added context manager support.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.6\n      Popen destructor now emits a :exc:`ResourceWarning` warning if the child\n      process is still running.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.8\n      Popen can use :func:`os.posix_spawn` in some cases for better\n      performance. On Windows Subsystem for Linux and QEMU User Emulation,\n      Popen constructor using :func:`os.posix_spawn` no longer raise an\n      exception on errors like missing program, but the child process fails\n      with a non-zero :attr:`~Popen.returncode`.\n\n\nExceptions\n^^^^^^^^^^\n\nExceptions raised in the child process, before the new program has started to\nexecute, will be re-raised in the parent.\n\nThe most common exception raised is :exc:`OSError`.  This occurs, for example,\nwhen trying to execute a non-existent file.  Applications should prepare for\n:exc:`OSError` exceptions. Note that, when ``shell=True``, :exc:`OSError`\nwill be raised by the child only if the selected shell itself was not found.\nTo determine if the shell failed to find the requested application, it is\nnecessary to check the return code or output from the subprocess.\n\nA :exc:`ValueError` will be raised if :class:`Popen` is called with invalid\narguments.\n\n:func:`check_call` and :func:`check_output` will raise\n:exc:`CalledProcessError` if the called process returns a non-zero return\ncode.\n\nAll of the functions and methods that accept a *timeout* parameter, such as\n:func:`run` and :meth:`Popen.communicate` will raise :exc:`TimeoutExpired` if\nthe timeout expires before the process exits.\n\nExceptions defined in this module all inherit from :exc:`SubprocessError`.\n\n.. versionadded:: 3.3\n   The :exc:`SubprocessError` base class was added.\n\n.. _subprocess-security:\n\nSecurity Considerations\n-----------------------\n\nUnlike some other popen functions, this library will not\nimplicitly choose to call a system shell.  This means that all characters,\nincluding shell metacharacters, can safely be passed to child processes.\nIf the shell is invoked explicitly, via ``shell=True``, it is the application's\nresponsibility to ensure that all whitespace and metacharacters are\nquoted appropriately to avoid\n`shell injection <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shell_injection#Shell_injection>`_\nvulnerabilities. On :ref:`some platforms <shlex-quote-warning>`, it is possible\nto use :func:`shlex.quote` for this escaping.\n\nOn Windows, batch files (:file:`*.bat` or :file:`*.cmd`) may be launched by the\noperating system in a system shell regardless of the arguments passed to this\nlibrary. This could result in arguments being parsed according to shell rules,\nbut without any escaping added by Python. If you are intentionally launching a\nbatch file with arguments from untrusted sources, consider passing\n``shell=True`` to allow Python to escape special characters. See :gh:`114539`\nfor additional discussion.\n\n\nPopen Objects\n-------------\n\nInstances of the :class:`Popen` class have the following methods:\n\n\n.. method:: Popen.poll()\n\n   Check if child process has terminated.  Set and return\n   :attr:`~Popen.returncode` attribute. Otherwise, returns ``None``.\n\n\n.. method:: Popen.wait(timeout=None)\n\n   Wait for child process to terminate.  Set and return\n   :attr:`~Popen.returncode` attribute.\n\n   If the process does not terminate after *timeout* seconds, raise a\n   :exc:`TimeoutExpired` exception.  It is safe to catch this exception and\n   retry the wait.\n\n   .. note::\n\n      This will deadlock when using ``stdout=PIPE`` or ``stderr=PIPE``\n      and the child process generates enough output to a pipe such that\n      it blocks waiting for the OS pipe buffer to accept more data.\n      Use :meth:`Popen.communicate` when using pipes to avoid that.\n\n   .. note::\n\n      When ``timeout`` is not ``None`` and the platform supports it, an\n      efficient event-driven mechanism is used to wait for process termination:\n\n      - Linux >= 5.3 uses :func:`os.pidfd_open` + :func:`select.poll`\n      - macOS and other BSD variants use :func:`select.kqueue` +\n        ``KQ_FILTER_PROC`` + ``KQ_NOTE_EXIT``\n      - Windows uses ``WaitForSingleObject``\n\n      If none of these mechanisms are available, the function falls back to a\n      busy loop (non-blocking call and short sleeps).\n\n   .. note::\n\n      Use the :mod:`asyncio` module for an asynchronous wait: see\n      :class:`asyncio.create_subprocess_exec`.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.3\n      *timeout* was added.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.15\n      if *timeout* is not ``None``, use efficient event-driven implementation\n      on Linux >= 5.3 and macOS / BSD.\n\n.. method:: Popen.communicate(input=None, timeout=None)\n\n   Interact with process: Send data to stdin.  Read data from stdout and stderr,\n   until end-of-file is reached.  Wait for process to terminate and set the\n   :attr:`~Popen.returncode` attribute.  The optional *input* argument should be\n   data to be sent to the child process, or ``None``, if no data should be sent\n   to the child.  If streams were opened in text mode, *input* must be a string.\n   Otherwise, it must be bytes.\n\n   :meth:`communicate` returns a tuple ``(stdout_data, stderr_data)``.\n   The data will be strings if streams were opened in text mode; otherwise,\n   bytes.\n\n   Note that if you want to send data to the process's stdin, you need to create\n   the Popen object with ``stdin=PIPE``.  Similarly, to get anything other than\n   ``None`` in the result tuple, you need to give ``stdout=PIPE`` and/or\n   ``stderr=PIPE`` too.\n\n   If the process does not terminate after *timeout* seconds, a\n   :exc:`TimeoutExpired` exception will be raised.  Catching this exception and\n   retrying communication will not lose any output.  Supplying *input* to a\n   subsequent post-timeout :meth:`communicate` call is in undefined behavior\n   and may become an error in the future.\n\n   The child process is not killed if the timeout expires, so in order to\n   cleanup properly a well-behaved application should kill the child process and\n   finish communication::\n\n      proc = subprocess.Popen(...)\n      try:\n          outs, errs = proc.communicate(timeout=15)\n      except TimeoutExpired:\n          proc.kill()\n          outs, errs = proc.communicate()\n\n   After a call to :meth:`~Popen.communicate` raises :exc:`TimeoutExpired`, do\n   not call :meth:`~Popen.wait`. Use an additional :meth:`~Popen.communicate`\n   call to finish handling pipes and populate the :attr:`~Popen.returncode`\n   attribute.\n\n   .. note::\n\n      The data read is buffered in memory, so do not use this method if the data\n      size is large or unlimited.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.3\n      *timeout* was added.\n\n\n.. method:: Popen.send_signal(signal)\n\n   Sends the signal *signal* to the child.\n\n   Do nothing if the process completed.\n\n   .. note::\n\n      On Windows, SIGTERM is an alias for :meth:`terminate`. CTRL_C_EVENT and\n      CTRL_BREAK_EVENT can be sent to processes started with a *creationflags*\n      parameter which includes ``CREATE_NEW_PROCESS_GROUP``.\n\n\n.. method:: Popen.terminate()\n\n   Stop the child. On POSIX OSs the method sends :py:const:`~signal.SIGTERM` to the\n   child. On Windows the Win32 API function :c:func:`!TerminateProcess` is called\n   to stop the child.\n\n\n.. method:: Popen.kill()\n\n   Kills the child. On POSIX OSs the function sends SIGKILL to the child.\n   On Windows :meth:`kill` is an alias for :meth:`terminate`.\n\n\nThe following attributes are also set by the class for you to access.\nReassigning them to new values is unsupported:\n\n.. attribute:: Popen.args\n\n   The *args* argument as it was passed to :class:`Popen` -- a\n   sequence of program arguments or else a single string.\n\n   .. versionadded:: 3.3\n\n.. attribute:: Popen.stdin\n\n   If the *stdin* argument was :data:`PIPE`, this attribute is a writeable\n   stream object as returned by :func:`open`. If the *encoding* or *errors*\n   arguments were specified or the *text* or *universal_newlines* argument\n   was ``True``, the stream is a text stream, otherwise it is a byte stream.\n   If the *stdin* argument was not :data:`PIPE`, this attribute is ``None``.\n\n\n.. attribute:: Popen.stdout\n\n   If the *stdout* argument was :data:`PIPE`, this attribute is a readable\n   stream object as returned by :func:`open`. Reading from the stream provides\n   output from the child process. If the *encoding* or *errors* arguments were\n   specified or the *text* or *universal_newlines* argument was ``True``, the\n   stream is a text stream, otherwise it is a byte stream. If the *stdout*\n   argument was not :data:`PIPE`, this attribute is ``None``.\n\n\n.. attribute:: Popen.stderr\n\n   If the *stderr* argument was :data:`PIPE`, this attribute is a readable\n   stream object as returned by :func:`open`. Reading from the stream provides\n   error output from the child process. If the *encoding* or *errors* arguments\n   were specified or the *text* or *universal_newlines* argument was ``True``, the\n   stream is a text stream, otherwise it is a byte stream. If the *stderr* argument\n   was not :data:`PIPE`, this attribute is ``None``.\n\n.. warning::\n\n   Use :meth:`~Popen.communicate` rather than :attr:`.stdin.write <Popen.stdin>`,\n   :attr:`.stdout.read <Popen.stdout>` or :attr:`.stderr.read <Popen.stderr>` to avoid\n   deadlocks due to any of the other OS pipe buffers filling up and blocking the\n   child process.\n\n\n.. attribute:: Popen.pid\n\n   The process ID of the child process.\n\n   Note that if you set the *shell* argument to ``True``, this is the process ID\n   of the spawned shell.\n\n\n.. attribute:: Popen.returncode\n\n   The child return code. Initially ``None``, :attr:`returncode` is set by\n   a call to the :meth:`poll`, :meth:`wait`, or :meth:`communicate` methods\n   if they detect that the process has terminated.\n\n   A ``None`` value in\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 48000:62026]\nctions comprised the high level API to\nsubprocess. You can now use :func:`run` in many cases, but lots of existing code\ncalls these functions.\n\n.. function:: call(args, *, stdin=None, stdout=None, stderr=None, \\\n                   shell=False, cwd=None, timeout=None, **other_popen_kwargs)\n\n   Run the command described by *args*.  Wait for command to complete, then\n   return the :attr:`~Popen.returncode` attribute.\n\n   Code needing to capture stdout or stderr should use :func:`run` instead::\n\n       run(...).returncode\n\n   To suppress stdout or stderr, supply a value of :data:`DEVNULL`.\n\n   The arguments shown above are merely some common ones.\n   The full function signature is the\n   same as that of the :class:`Popen` constructor - this function passes all\n   supplied arguments other than *timeout* directly through to that interface.\n\n   .. note::\n\n      Do not use ``stdout=PIPE`` or ``stderr=PIPE`` with this\n      function.  The child process will block if it generates enough\n      output to a pipe to fill up the OS pipe buffer as the pipes are\n      not being read from.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.3\n      *timeout* was added.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.12\n\n      Changed Windows shell search order for ``shell=True``. The current\n      directory and ``%PATH%`` are replaced with ``%COMSPEC%`` and\n      ``%SystemRoot%\\System32\\cmd.exe``. As a result, dropping a\n      malicious program named ``cmd.exe`` into a current directory no\n      longer works.\n\n.. function:: check_call(args, *, stdin=None, stdout=None, stderr=None, \\\n                         shell=False, cwd=None, timeout=None, \\\n                         **other_popen_kwargs)\n\n   Run command with arguments.  Wait for command to complete. If the return\n   code was zero then return, otherwise raise :exc:`CalledProcessError`. The\n   :exc:`CalledProcessError` object will have the return code in the\n   :attr:`~CalledProcessError.returncode` attribute.\n   If :func:`check_call` was unable to start the process it will propagate the exception\n   that was raised.\n\n   Code needing to capture stdout or stderr should use :func:`run` instead::\n\n       run(..., check=True)\n\n   To suppress stdout or stderr, supply a value of :data:`DEVNULL`.\n\n   The arguments shown above are merely some common ones.\n   The full function signature is the\n   same as that of the :class:`Popen` constructor - this function passes all\n   supplied arguments other than *timeout* directly through to that interface.\n\n   .. note::\n\n      Do not use ``stdout=PIPE`` or ``stderr=PIPE`` with this\n      function.  The child process will block if it generates enough\n      output to a pipe to fill up the OS pipe buffer as the pipes are\n      not being read from.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.3\n      *timeout* was added.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.12\n\n      Changed Windows shell search order for ``shell=True``. The current\n      directory and ``%PATH%`` are replaced with ``%COMSPEC%`` and\n      ``%SystemRoot%\\System32\\cmd.exe``. As a result, dropping a\n      malicious program named ``cmd.exe`` into a current directory no\n      longer works.\n\n\n.. function:: check_output(args, *, stdin=None, stderr=None, shell=False, \\\n                           cwd=None, encoding=None, errors=None, \\\n                           universal_newlines=None, timeout=None, text=None, \\\n                           **other_popen_kwargs)\n\n   Run command with arguments and return its output.\n\n   If the return code was non-zero it raises a :exc:`CalledProcessError`. The\n   :exc:`CalledProcessError` object will have the return code in the\n   :attr:`~CalledProcessError.returncode` attribute and any output in the\n   :attr:`~CalledProcessError.output` attribute.\n\n   This is equivalent to::\n\n       run(..., check=True, stdout=PIPE).stdout\n\n   The arguments shown above are merely some common ones.\n   The full function signature is largely the same as that of :func:`run` -\n   most arguments are passed directly through to that interface.\n   One API deviation from :func:`run` behavior exists: passing ``input=None``\n   will behave the same as ``input=b''`` (or ``input=''``, depending on other\n   arguments) rather than using the parent's standard input file handle.\n\n   By default, this function will return the data as encoded bytes. The actual\n   encoding of the output data may depend on the command being invoked, so the\n   decoding to text will often need to be handled at the application level.\n\n   This behaviour may be overridden by setting *text*, *encoding*, *errors*,\n   or *universal_newlines* to ``True`` as described in\n   :ref:`frequently-used-arguments` and :func:`run`.\n\n   To also capture standard error in the result, use\n   ``stderr=subprocess.STDOUT``::\n\n      >>> subprocess.check_output(\n      ...     \"ls non_existent_file; exit 0\",\n      ...     stderr=subprocess.STDOUT,\n      ...     shell=True)\n      'ls: non_existent_file: No such file or directory\\n'\n\n   .. versionadded:: 3.1\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.3\n      *timeout* was added.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.4\n      Support for the *input* keyword argument was added.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.6\n      *encoding* and *errors* were added.  See :func:`run` for details.\n\n   .. versionadded:: 3.7\n      *text* was added as a more readable alias for *universal_newlines*.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.12\n\n      Changed Windows shell search order for ``shell=True``. The current\n      directory and ``%PATH%`` are replaced with ``%COMSPEC%`` and\n      ``%SystemRoot%\\System32\\cmd.exe``. As a result, dropping a\n      malicious program named ``cmd.exe`` into a current directory no\n      longer works.\n\n\n.. _subprocess-replacements:\n\nReplacing Older Functions with the :mod:`!subprocess` Module\n------------------------------------------------------------\n\nIn this section, \"a becomes b\" means that b can be used as a replacement for a.\n\n.. note::\n\n   All \"a\" functions in this section fail (more or less) silently if the\n   executed program cannot be found; the \"b\" replacements raise :exc:`OSError`\n   instead.\n\n   In addition, the replacements using :func:`check_output` will fail with a\n   :exc:`CalledProcessError` if the requested operation produces a non-zero\n   return code. The output is still available as the\n   :attr:`~CalledProcessError.output` attribute of the raised exception.\n\nIn the following examples, we assume that the relevant functions have already\nbeen imported from the :mod:`!subprocess` module.\n\n\nReplacing :program:`/bin/sh` shell command substitution\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\n.. code-block:: bash\n\n   output=$(mycmd myarg)\n\nbecomes::\n\n   output = check_output([\"mycmd\", \"myarg\"])\n\nReplacing shell pipeline\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\n.. code-block:: bash\n\n   output=$(dmesg | grep hda)\n\nbecomes::\n\n   p1 = Popen([\"dmesg\"], stdout=PIPE)\n   p2 = Popen([\"grep\", \"hda\"], stdin=p1.stdout, stdout=PIPE)\n   p1.stdout.close()  # Allow p1 to receive a SIGPIPE if p2 exits.\n   output = p2.communicate()[0]\n\nThe ``p1.stdout.close()`` call after starting the p2 is important in order for\np1 to receive a SIGPIPE if p2 exits before p1.\n\nAlternatively, for trusted input, the shell's own pipeline support may still\nbe used directly:\n\n.. code-block:: bash\n\n   output=$(dmesg | grep hda)\n\nbecomes::\n\n   output = check_output(\"dmesg | grep hda\", shell=True)\n\n\nReplacing :func:`os.system`\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\n::\n\n   sts = os.system(\"mycmd\" + \" myarg\")\n   # becomes\n   retcode = call(\"mycmd\" + \" myarg\", shell=True)\n\nNotes:\n\n* Calling the program through the shell is usually not required.\n* The :func:`call` return value is encoded differently to that of\n  :func:`os.system`.\n\n* The :func:`os.system` function ignores SIGINT and SIGQUIT signals while\n  the command is running, but the caller must do this separately when\n  using the :mod:`!subprocess` module.\n\nA more realistic example would look like this::\n\n   try:\n       retcode = call(\"mycmd\" + \" myarg\", shell=True)\n       if retcode < 0:\n           print(\"Child was terminated by signal\", -retcode, file=sys.stderr)\n       else:\n           print(\"Child returned\", retcode, file=sys.stderr)\n   except OSError as e:\n       print(\"Execution failed:\", e, file=sys.stderr)\n\n\nReplacing the :func:`os.spawn <os.spawnl>` family\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\nP_NOWAIT example::\n\n   pid = os.spawnlp(os.P_NOWAIT, \"/bin/mycmd\", \"mycmd\", \"myarg\")\n   ==>\n   pid = Popen([\"/bin/mycmd\", \"myarg\"]).pid\n\nP_WAIT example::\n\n   retcode = os.spawnlp(os.P_WAIT, \"/bin/mycmd\", \"mycmd\", \"myarg\")\n   ==>\n   retcode = call([\"/bin/mycmd\", \"myarg\"])\n\nVector example::\n\n   os.spawnvp(os.P_NOWAIT, path, args)\n   ==>\n   Popen([path] + args[1:])\n\nEnvironment example::\n\n   os.spawnlpe(os.P_NOWAIT, \"/bin/mycmd\", \"mycmd\", \"myarg\", env)\n   ==>\n   Popen([\"/bin/mycmd\", \"myarg\"], env={\"PATH\": \"/usr/bin\"})\n\n\n\nReplacing :func:`os.popen`\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\nReturn code handling translates as follows::\n\n   pipe = os.popen(cmd, 'w')\n   ...\n   rc = pipe.close()\n   if rc is not None and rc >> 8:\n       print(\"There were some errors\")\n   ==>\n   process = Popen(cmd, stdin=PIPE)\n   ...\n   process.stdin.close()\n   if process.wait() != 0:\n       print(\"There were some errors\")\n\n\nLegacy Shell Invocation Functions\n---------------------------------\n\nThis module also provides the following legacy functions from the 2.x\n``commands`` module. These operations implicitly invoke the system shell and\nnone of the guarantees described above regarding security and exception\nhandling consistency are valid for these functions.\n\n.. function:: getstatusoutput(cmd, *, encoding=None, errors=None)\n\n   Return ``(exitcode, output)`` of executing *cmd* in a shell.\n\n   Execute the string *cmd* in a shell with :func:`check_output` and\n   return a 2-tuple ``(exitcode, output)``.\n   *encoding* and *errors* are used to decode output;\n   see the notes on :ref:`frequently-used-arguments` for more details.\n\n   A trailing newline is stripped from the output.\n   The exit code for the command can be interpreted as the return code\n   of subprocess.  Example::\n\n      >>> subprocess.getstatusoutput('ls /bin/ls')\n      (0, '/bin/ls')\n      >>> subprocess.getstatusoutput('cat /bin/junk')\n      (1, 'cat: /bin/junk: No such file or directory')\n      >>> subprocess.getstatusoutput('/bin/junk')\n      (127, 'sh: /bin/junk: not found')\n      >>> subprocess.getstatusoutput('/bin/kill $$')\n      (-15, '')\n\n   .. availability:: Unix, Windows.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.3.4\n      Windows support was added.\n\n      The function now returns (exitcode, output) instead of (status, output)\n      as it did in Python 3.3.3 and earlier.  exitcode has the same value as\n      :attr:`~Popen.returncode`.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.11\n      Added the *encoding* and *errors* parameters.\n\n.. function:: getoutput(cmd, *, encoding=None, errors=None)\n\n   Return output (stdout and stderr) of executing *cmd* in a shell.\n\n   Like :func:`getstatusoutput`, except the exit code is ignored and the return\n   value is a string containing the command's output.  Example::\n\n      >>> subprocess.getoutput('ls /bin/ls')\n      '/bin/ls'\n\n   .. availability:: Unix, Windows.\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.3.4\n      Windows support added\n\n   .. versionchanged:: 3.11\n      Added the *encoding* and *errors* parameters.\n\n\nNotes\n-----\n\n.. _subprocess-timeout-behavior:\n\nTimeout Behavior\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\nWhen using the ``timeout`` parameter in functions like :func:`run`,\n:meth:`Popen.wait`, or :meth:`Popen.communicate`,\nusers should be aware of the following behaviors:\n\n1. **Process Creation Delay**: The initial process creation itself cannot be interrupted\n   on many platform APIs. This means that even when specifying a timeout, you are not\n   guaranteed to see a timeout exception until at least after however long process\n   creation takes.\n\n2. **Extremely Small Timeout Values**: Setting very small timeout values (such as a few\n   milliseconds) may result in almost immediate :exc:`TimeoutExpired` exceptions because\n   process creation and system scheduling inherently require time.\n\n.. _converting-argument-sequence:\n\nConverting an argument sequence to a string on Windows\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\nOn Windows, an *args* sequence is converted to a string that can be parsed\nusing the following rules (which correspond to the rules used by the MS C\nruntime):\n\n1. Arguments are delimited by white space, which is either a\n   space or a tab.\n\n2. A string surrounded by double quotation marks is\n   interpreted as a single argument, regardless of white space\n   contained within.  A quoted string can be embedded in an\n   argument.\n\n3. A double quotation mark preceded by a backslash is\n   interpreted as a literal double quotation mark.\n\n4. Backslashes are interpreted literally, unless they\n   immediately precede a double quotation mark.\n\n5. If backslashes immediately precede a double quotation mark,\n   every pair of backslashes is interpreted as a literal\n   backslash.  If the number of backslashes is odd, the last\n   backslash escapes the next double quotation mark as\n   described in rule 3.\n\n\n.. seealso::\n\n   :mod:`shlex`\n      Module which provides function to parse and escape command lines.\n\n\n.. _disable_posix_spawn:\n\nDisable use of ``posix_spawn()``\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\nOn Linux, :mod:`!subprocess` defaults to using the ``vfork()`` system call\ninternally when it is safe to do so rather than ``fork()``. This greatly\nimproves performance.\n\n::\n\n   subprocess._USE_POSIX_SPAWN = False  # See CPython issue gh-NNNNNN.\n\nIt is safe to set this to false on any Python version. It will have no\neffect on older or newer versions where unsupported. Do not assume the attribute\nis available to read. Despite the name, a true value does not indicate the\ncorresponding function will be used, only that it may be.\n\nPlease file issues any time you have to use these private knobs with a way to\nreproduce the issue you were seeing. A user can guarantee that all thread blocks have started executing using\ncluster.sync()\nfrom\nCluster Group\nAPI.\n\nThe user also needs to ensure that all distributed shared memory operations happen before the exit of a thread block, e.g., if a remote thread block is trying to read a given thread block’s shared memory, user needs to ensure that the shared memory read by remote thread block is completed before it can exit.\nCUDA provides a mechanism to access to distributed shared memory, and applications can benefit from leveraging its capabilities. Lets look at a simple histogram computation and how to optimize it on the GPU using thread block cluster. A standard way of computing histograms is do the computation in the shared memory of each thread block and then perform global memory atomics. A limitation of this approach is the shared memory capacity. Once the histogram bins no longer fit in the shared memory, a user needs to directly compute histograms and hence the atomics in the global memory. With distributed shared memory, CUDA provides an intermediate step, where a depending on the histogram bins size, histogram can be computed in shared memory, distributed shared memory or global memory directly.\nThe CUDA kernel example below shows how to compute histograms in shared memory or distributed shared memory, depending on the number of histogram bins.\n#include\n<cooperative_groups.h>\n// Distributed Shared memory histogram kernel\n__global__\nvoid\nclusterHist_kernel\n(\nint\n*\nbins\n,\nconst\nint\nnbins\n,\nconst\nint\nbins_per_block\n,\nconst\nint\n*\n__restrict__\ninput\n,\nsize_t\narray_size\n)\n{\nextern\n__shared__\nint\nsmem\n[];\nnamespace\ncg\n=\ncooperative_groups\n;\nint\ntid\n=\ncg\n::\nthis_grid\n().\nthread_rank\n();\n// Cluster initialization, size and calculating local bin offsets.\ncg\n::\ncluster_group\ncluster\n=\ncg\n::\nthis_cluster\n();\nunsigned\nint\nclusterBlockRank\n=\ncluster\n.\nblock_rank\n();\nint\ncluster_size\n=\ncluster\n.\ndim_blocks\n().\nx\n;\nfor\n(\nint\ni\n=\nthreadIdx\n.\nx\n;\ni\n<\nbins_per_block\n;\ni\n+=\nblockDim\n.\nx\n)\n{\nsmem\n[\ni\n]\n=\n0\n;\n//Initialize shared memory histogram to zeros\n}\n// cluster synchronization ensures that shared memory is initialized to zero in\n// all thread blocks in the cluster. Vulkan memory object exported using VK_EXTERNAL\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 284000:288000]\n.\nPerformance Guidelines\n\n5.1.\nOverall Performance Optimization Strategies\n\nPerformance optimization revolves around four basic strategies:\nMaximize parallel execution to achieve maximum utilization;\nOptimize memory usage to achieve maximum memory throughput;\nOptimize instruction usage to achieve maximum instruction throughput;\nMinimize memory thrashing.\nWhich strategies will yield the best performance gain for a particular portion of an application depends on the performance limiters for that portion; optimizing instruction usage of a kernel that is mostly limited by memory accesses will not yield any significant performance gain, for example. Optimization efforts should therefore be constantly directed by measuring and monitoring the performance limiters, for example using the CUDA profiler. Also, comparing the floating-point operation throughput or memory throughput—whichever makes more sense—of a particular kernel to the corresponding peak theoretical throughput of the device indicates how much room for improvement there is for the kernel.\n5.2.\nMaximize Utilization\n\nTo maximize utilization the application should be structured in a way that it exposes as much parallelism as possible and efficiently maps this parallelism to the various components of the system to keep them busy most of the time.\n5.2.1.\nApplication Level\n\nAt a high level, the application should maximize parallel execution between the host, the devices, and the bus connecting the host to the devices, by using asynchronous functions calls and streams as described in\nAsynchronous Concurrent Execution\n. It should assign to each processor the type of work it does best: serial workloads to the host; parallel workloads to the devices.\nFor the parallel workloads, at points in the algorithm where parallelism is broken because some threads need to synchronize in order to share data with each other, there are two cases: Either these threads belong to the same block, in which case they should use\n__syncthreads()\nand share data through shared memory within the same kernel invocation, or they belong to different blocks, in which case they must share data through global memory using two separate kernel invocations, one for writing to and one for reading from global memory. The second case is much less optimal since it adds the overhead of extra kernel invocations and global memory traffic. Its occurrence should therefore be minimized by mapping the algorithm to the CUDA programming model in such a way that the computations that require inter-thread communication are performed within a single thread block as much as possible.\n5.2.2.\nDevice Level\n\nAt a lower level, the application should maximize parallel execution between the multiprocessors of a device.\nMultiple kernels can execute concurrently on a device, so maximum utilization can also be achieved by using streams to enable enough kernels to execute concurrently as described in\nAsynchronous Concurrent Execution\n.\n5.2.3.\nMultiprocessor Level\n\nAt an even lower level, the application should maximize parallel execution between the various functional units within a multiprocessor.\nAs described in\nHardware Multithreading\n, a GPU multiprocessor primarily relies on thread-level parallelism to maximize utilization of its functional units. Utilization is therefore directly linked to the number of resident warps. At every instruction issue time, a warp scheduler selects an instruction that is ready to execute. This instruction can be another independent instruction of the same warp, exploiting instruction-level parallelism, or more commonly an instruction of another warp, exploiting thread-level parallelism. If a ready to execute instruction is selected it is issued to the\nactive\nthreads of the warp. The number of clock cycles it takes for a warp to be ready to execute its next instruction is called the\nlatency\n, and full utilization is achieved when all warp schedulers always have some instruction to issue for some warp at e\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 456000:460000]\ncooperative_groups::wait\n.\nThe complete API documentation of the\ncuda::memcpy_async\noverloads for\ncuda::barrier\nand\ncuda::pipeline\nis provided in the\nlibcudacxx API\ndocumentation along with some examples.\nThe API documentation of\ncooperative_groups::memcpy_async\nis provided in the\nCooperative Groups\nsection.\nThe\nmemcpy_async\nAPIs that use\ncuda::barrier\nand\ncuda::pipeline\nrequire compute capability 7.0 or higher. On devices with compute capability 8.0 or higher,\nmemcpy_async\noperations from global to shared memory can benefit from hardware acceleration.\n7.27.2.\nCopy and Compute Pattern - Staging Data Through Shared Memory\n\nCUDA applications often employ a\ncopy and compute\npattern that:\nfetches data from global memory,\nstores data to shared memory, and\nperforms computations on shared memory data, and potentially writes results back to global memory.\nThe following sections illustrate how this pattern can be expressed without and with the\nmemcpy_async\nfeature:\nWithout memcpy_async\nintroduces an example that does not overlap computation with data movement and uses an intermediate register to copy data.\nWith memcpy_async\nimproves the previous example by introducing the\nmemcpy_async\nand the\ncuda::memcpy_async\nAPIs to directly copy data from global to shared memory without using intermediate registers.\nAsynchronous Data Copies using cuda::barrier\nshows memcpy with cooperative groups and barrier.\nSingle-Stage Asynchronous Data Copies using cuda::pipeline\nshows memcpy with single stage pipeline.\nMulti-Stage Asynchronous Data Copies using cuda::pipeline\nshows memcpy with multi stage pipeline.\n7.27.3.\nWithout\nmemcpy_async\n\nWithout\nmemcpy_async\n, the\ncopy\nphase of the\ncopy and compute\npattern is expressed as\nshared[local_idx]\n=\nglobal[global_idx]\n. This global to shared memory copy is expanded to a read from global memory into a register, followed by a write to shared memory from the register.\nWhen this pattern occurs within an iterative algorithm, each thread block needs to synchronize after the\nshared[local_idx]\n=\nglobal[global_idx]\nassignment, to ensure all writes to shared memory have completed before the compute phase can begin. The thread block also needs to synchronize again after the compute phase, to prevent overwriting shared memory before all threads have completed their computations. This pattern is illustrated in the following code snippet.\n#include\n<cooperative_groups.h>\n__device__\nvoid\ncompute\n(\nint\n*\nglobal_out\n,\nint\nconst\n*\nshared_in\n)\n{\n// Computes using all byte aligned.\nShared memory barrier address\nMust be 8 byte aligned (this is guaranteed by\ncuda::barrier\n).\nSize of transfer\nMust be a multiple of 16 bytes.\n7.29.2.\nUsing TMA to transfer multi-dimensional arrays\n\nThe primary difference between the one-dimensional and multi-dimensional case is\n\nthat a tensor map must be created on the host and passed to the CUDA kernel.\n\nThis Creating a tensor map requires many parameters. Among\n\nthem are the base pointer to an array in global memory, the size of the array\n\n(in number of elements), the stride from one row to the next (in bytes), the\n\nsize of the shared memory buffer (in number of elements). The code below creates\n\na tensor map to describe a two-dimensional row-major array of size\nGMEM_HEIGHT\nx\nGMEM_WIDTH\n. Note the order of the parameters: the fastest moving dimension\n\ncomes first.\nCUtensorMap\ntensor_map\n{};\n// rank is the number of dimensions of the array.\nconstexpr\nuint32_t\nrank\n=\n2\n;\nuint64_t\nsize\n[\nrank\n]\n=\n{\nGMEM_WIDTH\n,\nGMEM_HEIGHT\n};\n// The stride is the number of bytes to traverse from the first element of one row to the next.\n// It must be a multiple of 16.\nuint64_t\nstride\n[\nrank\n-\n1\n]\n=\n{\nGMEM_WIDTH\n*\nsizeof\n(\nint\n)};\n// The box_size is the size of the shared memory buffer that is used as the\n// destination of a TMA transfer.\nuint32_t\nbox_size\n[\nrank\n]\n=\n{\nSMEM_WIDTH\n,\nSMEM_HEIGHT\n};\n// The distance between elements in units of sizeof(element). There are three ways to make a tensor map accessible to\n\ndevice code. The recommended approach is to pass the tensor map as a const\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 516000:536000]\n eight-way bank conflict when storing. Finally, the transpose buffer is written back to global memory.\nTo avoid bank conflicts, the\nCU_TENSOR_MAP_SWIZZLE_128B\nlayout can be used. This layout matches the 128 bytes row length and\n\nchanges the shared memory layout in a way that both the column wise and row wise access don’t require the same banks per transaction.\nThe two tables,\nFigure 26\nand\nFigure 27\n,  below show the normal and the swizzled shared memory layout of the 8x8 matrix of type\nint4\nand\n\nits transpose matrix. The colors indicate which of the eight groups of four banks the matrix element is mapped to, and\n\nthe margin row and margin column list the global memory row and column indices. The entries show the shared memory\n\nindices of the 16-byte matrix elements.\nFigure 26\nIn the shared memory data layout without swizzle, the shared memory indices are equivalent to the global memory indices.\n\nPer load instruction, one row is read and stored in a column of the transpose buffer. Since all matrix elements of the\n\ncolumn in the transpose fall in the same bank, the store must be serialized, resulting in eight store transactions, giving\n\nan eight-way bank conflict per stored column.\n\nFigure 27\nThe shared memory data layout with\nCU_TENSOR_MAP_SWIZZLE_128B\nswizzle. One row is stored in a column, each matrix\n\nelement is from a different bank for both the rows and columns, and so without any bank conflicts.\n\n__global__\nvoid\nkernel_tma\n(\nconst\n__grid_constant__\nCUtensorMap\ntensor_map\n)\n{\n// The destination shared memory buffer of a bulk tensor operation\n// with the 128-byte swizzle mode, it should be 1024 bytes aligned.\n__shared__\nalignas\n(\n1024\n)\nint4\nsmem_buffer\n[\n8\n][\n8\n];\n__shared__\nalignas\n(\n1024\n)\nint4\nsmem_buffer_tr\n[\n8\n][\n8\n];\n// Initialize shared memory barrier\n#pragma nv_diag_suppress static_var_with_dynamic_init\n__shared__\nbarrier\nbar\n;\nif\n(\nthreadIdx\n.\nx\n==\n0\n)\n{\ninit\n(\n&\nbar\n,\nblockDim\n.\nx\n);\ncde\n::\nfence_proxy_async_shared_cta\n();\n}\n__syncthreads\n();\nbarrier\n::\narrival_token\ntoken\n;\nif\n(\nthreadIdx\n.\nx\n==\n0\n)\n{\n// Initiate bulk tensor copy from global to shared memory,\n// in the same way as without swizzle.\ncde\n::\ncp_async_bulk_tensor_2d_global_to_shared\n(\n&\nsmem_buffer\n,\n&\ntensor_map\n,\n0\n,\n0\n,\nbar\n);\ntoken\n=\ncuda\n::\ndevice\n::\nbarrier_arrive_tx\n(\nbar\n,\n1\n,\nsizeof\n(\nsmem_buffer\n));\n}\nelse\n{\ntoken\n=\nbar\n.\narrive\n();\n}\nbar\n.\nwait\n(\nstd\n::\nmove\n(\ntoken\n));\n/* Matrix transpose\n*  When using the normal shared memory layout, there are eight\n*  8-way shared memory bank conflict when storing to the transpose.\n*  When enabling the 128-byte swizzle pattern and using the according access pattern,\n*  they are eliminated both for load and store. */\nfor\n(\nint\nsidx_j\n=\nthreadIdx\n.\nx\n;\nsidx_j\n<\n8\n;\nsidx_j\n+=\nblockDim\n.\nx\n){\nfor\n(\nint\nsidx_i\n=\n0\n;\nsidx_i\n<\n8\n;\n++\nsidx_i\n){\nconst\nint\nswiz_j_idx\n=\n(\nsidx_i\n%\n8\n)\n^\nsidx_j\n;\nconst\nint\nswiz_i_idx_tr\n=\n(\nsidx_j\n%\n8\n)\n^\nsidx_i\n;\nsmem_buffer_tr\n[\nsidx_j\n][\nswiz_i_idx_tr\n]\n=\nsmem_buffer\n[\nsidx_i\n][\nswiz_j_idx\n];\n}\n}\n// Wait for shared memory writes to be visible to TMA engine.\ncde\n::\nfence_proxy_async_shared_cta\n();\n__syncthreads\n();\n/* Initiate TMA transfer to copy the transposed shared memory buffer back to global memory,\n* it will 'unswizzle' the data. */\nif\n(\nthreadIdx\n.\nx\n==\n0\n)\n{\ncde\n::\ncp_async_bulk_tensor_2d_shared_to_global\n(\n&\ntensor_map\n,\n0\n,\n0\n,\n&\nsmem_buffer_tr\n);\ncde\n::\ncp_async_bulk_commit_group\n();\ncde\n::\ncp_async_bulk_wait_group_read\n<\n0\n>\n();\n}\n// Destroy barrier\nif\n(\nthreadIdx\n.\nx\n==\n0\n)\n{\n(\n&\nbar\n)\n->~\nbarrier\n();\n}\n}\n// --------------------------------- main ----------------------------------------\nint\nmain\n(){\n...\nvoid\n*\ntensor_ptr\n=\nd_data\n;\nCUtensorMap\ntensor_map\n{};\n// rank is the number of dimensions of the array.\nconstexpr\nuint32_t\nrank\n=\n2\n;\n// global memory size\nuint64_t\nsize\n[\nrank\n]\n=\n{\n4\n*\n8\n,\n8\n};\n// global memory stride, must be a multiple of 16.\nuint64_t\nstride\n[\nrank\n-\n1\n]\n=\n{\n8\n*\nsizeof\n(\nint4\n)};\n// The inner shared memory box dimension in bytes, equal to the swizzle span.\nuint32_t\nbox_size\n[\nrank\n]\n=\n{\n4\n*\n8\n,\n8\n};\nuint32_t\nelem_stride\n[\nrank\n]\n=\n{\n1\n,\n1\n};\n// Create the tensor descriptor.\nCUresult\nres\n=\ncuTensorMapEncodeTiled\n(\n&\ntensor_map\n,\n// CUtensorMap *tensorMap,\nCUtensorMapDataType\n::\nCU_TENSOR_MAP_DATA_TYPE_INT32\n,\nrank\n,\n// cuuint32_t tensorRank,\ntensor_ptr\n,\n// void *globalAddress,\nsize\n,\n// const cuuint64_t *globalDim,\nstride\n,\n// const cuuint64_t *globalStrides,\nbox_size\n,\n// const cuuint32_t *boxDim,\nelem_stride\n,\n// const cuuint32_t *elementStrides,\nCUtensorMapInterleave\n::\nCU_TENSOR_MAP_INTERLEAVE_NONE\n,\n// Using a swizzle pattern of 128 bytes.\nCUtensorMapSwizzle\n::\nCU_TENSOR_MAP_SWIZZLE_128B\n,\nCUtensorMapL2promotion\n::\nCU_TENSOR_MAP_L2_PROMOTION_NONE\n,\nCUtensorMapFloatOOBfill\n::\nCU_TENSOR_MAP_FLOAT_OOB_FILL_NONE\n);\nkernel_tma\n<<<\n1\n,\n8\n>>>\n(\ntensor_map\n);\n...\n}\nRemark.\nThis example is supposed to show the use of swizzle and ‘as-is’ is not performant nor does it scale beyond the given dimensions.\nExplanation.\nDuring data transfer, the TMA engine shuffles the data according to the swizzle pattern, as described in the following\n\ntables. These swizzle patterns define the mapping of the 16-byte chunks along the swizzle width to subgroups of four banks.\n\nIt is of type\nCUtensorMapSwizzle\nand has four options: none, 32 bytes, 64 bytes and 128 bytes. Note that the shared memory box’s\n\ninner dimension must be less or equal to the span of the swizzle pattern.\n7.29.3.2.\nThe Swizzle Modes\n\nAs previously mentioned, there are four swizzle modes. The following tables show the different swizzle patterns, including the relation of the new\n\nshared memory indices. The tables define the mapping of the 16-byte chunks along the 128 bytes to eight subgroups of four banks.\nFigure 28\nAn Overview of TMA Swizzle Patterns\n\nConsiderations.\nWhen applying a TMA swizzle pattern, it is crucial to adhere to specific memory requirements:\nGlobal memory alignment:\nGlobal memory must be aligned to 128 bytes.\nShared memory alignment:\nFor simplicity shared memory should be aligned according to the number of bytes after which the swizzle pattern repeats. When the shared memory buffer is not aligned by the number of bytes by which the swizzle pattern repeats itself, there is an offset between the swizzle pattern and the shared memory.\n\nSee\ncomment\n, below.\nInner dimension:\nThe inner dimension of the shared memory block must meet the size requirements specified in table\nTable 10\n. If these\n\nrequirements are not met, the instruction is considered invalid. Additionally, if the swizzle width exceeds the inner dimension,\n\nensure that the shared memory is allocated to accommodate the full swizzle width.\nGranularity:\nThe granularity of swizzle mapping is fixed at 16 bytes. This means that data is organized and accessed in chunks\n\nof 16 bytes, which must be considered when planning memory layout and access patterns.\nSwizzle Pattern Pointer Offset Computation\n. Here, we describe how to determine the offset between the swizzle pattern and the shared memory, when the shared memory buffer is not aligned by the number of bytes by which the swizzle pattern repeats itself.\n\nWhen using TMA, the shared memory is required to be aligned to 128 bytes. To find how many times the shared memory buffer relative to the swizzle pattern is shifted by that, apply the corresponding offset formula.\nTable 9\nSwizzle Pattern Pointer Offset Formula and Index Relation\n\nSwizzle Mode\nOffset Formula\nIndex Relation\nCU_TENSOR_MAP_SWIZZLE_128B\n(reinterpret_cast\n<uintptr_t>(smem_ptr)/128)%8\nsmem[y][x]\n<->\nsmem[y][((y+offset)%8)^x]\nCU_TENSOR_MAP_SWIZZLE_64B\n(reinterpret_cast\n<uintptr_t>(smem_ptr)/128)%4\nsmem[y][x]\n<->\nsmem[y][((y+offset)%4)^x]\nCU_TENSOR_MAP_SWIZZLE_32B\n(reinterpret_cast\n<uintptr_t>(smem_ptr)/128)%2\nsmem[y][x]\n<->\nsmem[y][((y+offset)%2)^x]\nIn\nFigure 28\n, this offset represents the initial row offset, thus, in the swizzle index calculation, it is added to the row index\ny\n.\n\nThe following snippet shows how to acces the swizzled shared memory in the\nCU_TENSOR_MAP_SWIZZLE_128B\nmode.\ndata_t\n*\nsmem_ptr\n=\n&\nsmem\n[\n0\n][\n0\n];\nint\noffset\n=\n(\nreinterpret_cast\n<\nuintptr_t\n>\n(\nsmem_ptr\n)\n/\n128\n)\n%\n8\n;\nsmem\n[\ny\n][((\ny\n+\noffset\n)\n%\n8\n)\n^\nx\n]\n=\n...\nSummary.\nThe following table\nRequirements and properties of the different swizzle patterns for Compute Capability 9\nsummarizes the requirements and properties of the different swizzle patterns for Compute bytes\nCU_TENSOR_MAP_SWIZZLE_64B\n64 bytes\n<=64 bytes\n512 bytes\n128 bytes\n128 bytes\nCU_TENSOR_MAP_SWIZZLE_32B\n32 bytes\n<=32 bytes\n256 bytes\n128 bytes\n128 bytes\nCU_TENSOR_MAP_SWIZZLE_NONE\n\n(default)\n128 bytes\n16 bytes\n7.30.\nEncoding a Tensor Map on Device\n\nPrevious sections have described how to create a tensor map on the host using the CUDA driver API.\nThis section explains how to encode a tiled-type tensor map on device. This is useful in situations where the typical\n\nway of transferring the tensor map (using\nconst\n__grid_constant__\nkernel parameters) is undesirable, for instance,\n\nwhen processing a batch of tensors of various sizes in a single kernel launch.\nThe recommended pattern is as follows:\nCreate a tensor map “template”,\ntemplate_tensor_map\n, using the Driver API on the host.\nIn a device kernel, copy the\ntemplate_tensor_map\n, modify the copy, store in global memory, and appropriately fence.\nUse the tensor map in a kernel with appropriate fencing.\nThe high-level code structure is as follows:\n// Initialize device context:\nCUDA_CHECK\n(\ncudaDeviceSynchronize\n());\n// Create a tensor map template using the cuTensorMapEncodeTiled driver function\nCUtensorMap\ntemplate_tensor_map\n=\nmake_tensormap_template\n();\n// Allocate tensor map and tensor in global memory\nCUtensorMap\n*\nglobal_tensor_map\n;\nCUDA_CHECK\n(\ncudaMalloc\n(\n&\nglobal_tensor_map\n,\nsizeof\n(\nCUtensorMap\n)));\nchar\n*\nglobal_buf\n;\nCUDA_CHECK\n(\ncudaMalloc\n(\n&\nglobal_buf\n,\n8\n*\n256\n));\n// Fill global buffer with data.\nfill_global_buf\n<<<\n1\n,\n1\n>>>\n(\nglobal_buf\n);\n// Define the parameters of the tensor map that will be created on device.\ntensormap_params\np\n{};\np\n.\nglobal_address\n=\nglobal_buf\n;\np\n.\nrank\n=\n2\n;\np\n.\nbox_dim\n[\n0\n]\n=\n128\n;\n// The box in shared memory has half the width of the full buffer\np\n.\nbox_dim\n[\n1\n]\n=\n4\n;\n// The box in shared memory has half the height of the full buffer\np\n.\nglobal_dim\n[\n0\n]\n=\n256\n;\n//\np\n.\nglobal_dim\n[\n1\n]\n=\n8\n;\n//\np\n.\nglobal_stride\n[\n0\n]\n=\n256\n;\n//\np\n.\nelement_stride\n[\n0\n]\n=\n1\n;\n//\np\n.\nelement_stride\n[\n1\n]\n=\n1\n;\n//\n// Encode global_tensor_map on device:\nencode_tensor_map\n<<<\n1\n,\n32\n>>>\n(\ntemplate_tensor_map\n,\np\n,\nglobal_tensor_map\n);\n// Use it from another kernel:\nconsume_tensor_map\n<<<\n1\n,\n1\n>>>\n(\nglobal_tensor_map\n);\n// Check for errors:\nCUDA_CHECK\n(\ncudaDeviceSynchronize\n());\nThe following sections  describe the high-level steps. Throughout the examples, the following\ntensormap_params\nstruct contains the new values of the fields to be updated. It is included here to reference when reading the examples.\nstruct\ntensormap_params\n{\nvoid\n*\nglobal_address\n;\nint\nrank\n;\nuint32_t\nbox_dim\n[\n5\n];\nuint64_t\nglobal_dim\n[\n5\n];\nsize_t\nglobal_stride\n[\n4\n];\nuint32_t\nelement_stride\n[\n5\n];\n};\n7.30.1.\nDevice-side Encoding and Modification of a Tensor Map\n\nThe recommended process of encoding a tensor map in global memory proceeds as follows.\nPass an existing tensor map, the\ntemplate_tensor_map\n, to the kernel. In contrast to kernels that use\n\nthe tensor map in a\ncp.async.bulk.tensor\ninstruction, this may be done in any way: a pointer to global\n\nmemory, kernel parameter, a\n__const___\nvariable, and so on.\nCopy-initialize a tensor map in shared memory with the template_tensor_map value.\nModify the tensor map in shared memory using the\ncuda::ptx::tensormap_replace\nfunctions. These functions wrap the\ntensormap.replace\nPTX instruction, which can be used to modify any field of a tiled-type tensor map, including the\n\nbase address, size, stride, and so on.\nUsing the\ncuda::ptx::tensormap_copy_fenceproxy\nfunction, copy the modified tensor map from shared memory to global memory and perform any necessary fencing.\nThe following code contains a kernel that follows these steps. For completeness, it modifies all the fields\n\nof the tensor map. Typically, a kernel will modify just a few fields.\nIn this kernel,\ntemplate_tensor_map\nis passed as a kernel parameter. This is the preferred way of moving\ntemplate_tensor_map\nfrom the host to the device. If the kernel is intended to update an existing tensor map in device memory, it can take a\n\npointer to the existing tensor map to modify.\nNote\nThe format of the tensor map may change over time. Therefore, the\ncuda::ptx::tensormap_replace\nfunctions and corresponding\ntensormap.replace.tile\nPTX instructions are marked as specific to sm_90a. To use them, compile using\nnvcc\n-arch\nsm_90a\n....\n.\nTip\nOn sm_90a, a zero-initialized buffer in shared memory may also be used as the initial tensor map value. This\n\nenables encoding a tensor map purely on device, without using the driver API to encode the\ntemplate_tensor_map\nvalue\n.\nNote\nOn-device modification is only supported for tiled-type tensor maps; other tensor map types cannot be modified on device.
